1	# 229 — Data box: a language-agnostic file source (and a paired file sink)
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	The `data` kind is recognized by the schema (`call` and `data` are
9	the two valid kinds) and accepted by the loader, but it has no
10	runtime semantics distinct from `call`. It carries a `ref` and an
11	`fn` like a call box and runs through whichever language driver
12	matches the file extension. The only difference is the green badge
13	on the canvas — pure cosmetic.
14	
15	A user reading the editor reasonably expects `data` to mean "read
16	some data, emit it" — "if it walks like a duck…" — but the current
17	implementation doesn't deliver that. `libs/files.lua::read_text`
18	covers the actual "read a text file" need (issue 216, complete) via
19	a regular call box.
20	
21	## Intended behavior
22	
23	Repurpose the `data` kind into a **language-agnostic file source**.
24	A data box has:
25	- `ref` — an absolute or map-relative path to a file on disk.
26	- **No `fn`** — the dispatch layer is the function.
27	- **No inputs** — emits whenever its successors fire.
28	- A single output wire carrying the file's contents.
29	
30	The runtime reads the file at the dispatch layer, encodes the bytes
31	as a string (UTF-8), and pushes the value onto the output wire. No
32	language driver is invoked. Same shape as the iterator box (issue
33	221): kind says "call," presence of a routing field marks the box
34	as a primitive the dispatch layer handles directly.
35	
36	### Why language-agnostic matters
37	
38	A user writing a Bash function that needs file contents shouldn't
39	have to wire through a Lua read box — and vice versa. A file's
40	bytes are a string; every language consumes a string; the
41	read-and-emit step has no language-specific anything. Pulling it
42	into the dispatch layer makes it available identically from every
43	language.
44	
45	It also keeps the graph readable: a user reading a map sees
46	`config.json → parse → ...` and immediately knows where the data
47	came from. The current alternative — a call box pointing at
48	`files.read_text` — works but adds a layer of indirection that
49	buries what the user is doing.
50	
51	### Paired sink: `kind: "sink"` (or similar) for writing files
52	
53	The complement is a language-agnostic file sink. A user wires a
54	string into it; the dispatch layer writes the string to the
55	configured path. Inputs:
56	- `path` (string) — destination on disk
57	- `text` (string) — content to write
58	
59	No outputs (per issue 226's sink semantics). No `ref` or `fn`. The
60	kind name needs picking — `sink` is taken metaphorically already;
61	`file_write` is unambiguous; `write` is short. Defer the bikeshed.
62	
63	Both the source and the sink atomic-write where it matters: write
64	to `path.tmp`, fsync, rename to `path`. Standard pattern; matches
65	how `soramech-data` handles its writes.
66	
67	### Schema
68	
69	For the source:
70	```json
71	{
72	  "id": "config",
73	  "kind": "data",
74	  "path": "config.json",
75	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
76	}
77	```
78	
79	`ref` is gone — repurposed as `path` so the field name says what it
80	is at the new abstraction level (read a file at this path, not call
81	a function at this ref). Inputs are absent; outputs are absent (the
82	single-output rule still gives one wire).
83	
84	For the sink, the kind name TBD; data layout:
85	```json
86	{
87	  "id": "save",
88	  "kind": "file_write",
89	  "inputs": [
90	    {"name": "path",  "type": "string"},
91	    {"name": "text",  "type": "string"}
92	  ],
93	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
94	}
95	```
96	
97	No `path` field on the box — it's wired in. Lets the user compute
98	paths upstream (e.g. by a function that builds a timestamped name).
99	Could also add an optional `path` field for the static case where
100	the user just wants a file written to a fixed location.
101	
102	### Concerns about language-agnostic file IO
103	
104	The original "data files have language semantics" concern (paraphrasing
105	from prior conversations: "different languages store strings
106	differently") doesn't apply here. The runtime owns the read/write,
107	not any language; it always emits bytes-as-UTF-8 string and always
108	accepts the same shape on the way in. Languages each receive a
109	plain string from the wire and turn it into whatever their native
110	string type is — that conversion is already part of the language
111	spec contract (issue 303).
112	
113	There is one wrinkle: **binary data**. UTF-8 encoding of arbitrary
114	bytes can fail (invalid sequences). Two ways to handle:
115	1. Reject non-UTF-8 files at read time with a clear error. Forces
116	   the user to acknowledge the binary case explicitly.
117	2. Pass through as raw bytes in some marker (base64?) and let the
118	   downstream box decode. Adds complexity most users won't need.
119	
120	Recommendation: option 1 for now. Binary data is out of scope
121	until someone needs it; the error surfaces clearly so the user
122	can switch to a language-specific binary reader.
123	
124	### JSON parsing
125	
126	The user asked whether parsing should happen in the data box or in
127	a downstream box. Recommendation: **the data box is text-only**;
128	JSON parsing belongs to a downstream call (Lua: `dkjson.decode`,
129	language-agnostic option: a parse box). Reasons:
130	- Parsing a JSON file produces a structured object, not a string;
131	  the wire protocol between boxes is currently strings (per the
132	  language spec contract). Moving structured values across the
133	  wire is a separate change with bigger implications.
134	- Different downstream uses want different parsings (decoded
135	  table, JSON path extraction, schema validation). Letting the
136	  data box stay narrow keeps it composable.
137	
138	## Migration
139	
140	Existing `data` boxes (if any) currently behave like `call`. They
141	have a `ref` and `fn`. Two options:
142	1. Convert `data` → `call` for old boxes, then redefine `data` as
143	   the new file-source shape.
144	2. Leave old `data` boxes as-is; the editor refuses to interpret
145	   them as the new shape until the user explicitly converts.
146	
147	Option 1 is cleaner since the existing usage was incidental
148	(nobody was treating `data` as semantically distinct). A small
149	migration in `src/003-loader.lua` rewrites old `data` boxes on
150	read.
151	
152	## Suggested implementation sequence
153	
154	1. **Schema** (`src/001-schema.lua`): allow `data` boxes with
155	   `path` and no `ref`/`fn`; the new kind for writes (TBD name)
156	   takes inputs `path` and `text` and no `ref`/`fn`.
157	2. **Loader** (`src/003-loader.lua`): convert legacy `data` boxes
158	   (`kind=data` with `ref`/`fn`) to `kind=call`. Validate the new
159	   shapes.
160	3. **Phase 2 executor** (`src/004-executor.lua`): handle the new
161	   kinds directly — read the file and push to output for the
162	   source; receive inputs and write to disk for the sink. No
163	   driver invocation either way.
164	4. **Phase 3 dispatch** (issue 304): same handling at the dispatch
165	   layer. Source and sink are dispatch-layer primitives, alongside
166	   iterator and comparator routing.
167	5. **Inspector** (`assets/js/004-inspector.js`): when `kind=data`,
168	   show a `path` text input in place of `ref`/`fn`/inputs. When
169	   `kind=file_write` (or final name), show the inputs section as
170	   usual but no ref/fn/output.
171	6. **Box rendering** (`assets/js/002-boxes.js`): keep the green
172	   badge for the source; pick a color for the sink.
173	7. **Documentation**: update `docs/001-architecture.md` to describe
174	   the new `data` semantics. The old "library box" notes (if any)
175	   need a small rewrite.
176	
177	## Open questions
178	
179	- **Sink kind name**: `file_write`, `write`, `out`, `save`,
180	  `to_file`, `sink`. Lean toward `file_write` for "says what it is."
181	- **Static vs wired path on the sink**: support both, or pick one?
182	  Wired path is more flexible; static path is a common case worth
183	  one fewer wire. Probably accept both — `path` in the box JSON
184	  is used as the default; if a wire is connected to the `path`
185	  input, it overrides.
186	- **Binary data**: explicitly out of scope per above; revisit when
187	  a real use case appears.
188	- **Atomic-write vs simple write**: atomic-write (`tmp + rename`)
189	  is the safe default. If a user wants log-append semantics,
190	  that's a different sink kind (or a flag on this one).
191	
192	## Relevant files
193	
194	- `src/001-schema.lua` — kind validation, new shape
195	- `src/003-loader.lua` — legacy `data` migration
196	- `src/004-executor.lua` — phase 2 runtime handling
197	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — kind-specific inspector layout
198	- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — render
199	- `docs/001-architecture.md` — update box-kinds section
200	- `issues/completed/216-read-file-box.md` — Lua-side equivalent
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

1	# 224 — Editable port names and canvas-side value editing
2	
3	## Status
4	complete (canvas-overlay reverted; name editing in inspector,
5	value moved below name+variadic row)
6	
7	## Scope changed twice
8	
9	**First pass** (canvas-overlay version): moved the editable name
10	input out of the inspector and onto a DOM overlay floating next to
11	the box's input dot.
12	
13	**Why that was wrong**:
14	1. The `<input>` overlay captured pointer events on the box body;
15	   users dragging a box from near a port dot landed on the field
16	   instead and entered text-edit mode.
17	2. The overlay text was frozen at one screen size — it didn't
18	   scale with zoom.
19	
20	**Final design**: keep names on the canvas as plain `ctx.fillText`
21	labels (scale with zoom, ignore clicks). Names are also editable in
22	the inspector — same identity displayed in two places, so the user
23	sees what's there at a glance and can edit when the inspector is
24	open. The inspector port row now spans two lines: name + variadic
25	toggle on top, value on a wider second line below, so the value
26	(often the longest entry) gets the full sidebar width.
27	
28	## Implementation notes
29	
30	`assets/js/004-inspector.js::mk_port_display` rebuilt:
31	- Top row: name `<input>` + optional `:type` span + variadic button.
32	- Bottom row: full-width value `<input>`.
33	- Variadic-slot names disable the name input — variadic ops own
34	  the `<base>_<N>` pattern, and freeform renames break the group's
35	  membership tracking. The disabled state visually softens the
36	  field (no border, dim color) so it reads as "managed by the
37	  toggle" rather than "broken."
38	- Name commit on blur / Enter; Escape reverts to the saved name.
39	
40	`004-inspector.js` exports `rename_port(box, port_idx, new_name)`:
41	- Validation: non-empty, identifier-shaped (`^[A-Za-z_]\w*$`),
42	  unique within the box.
43	- Rewrites `to_input` on every wire targeting the port (both
44	  endpoints, mutating in place per the stale-cache lesson from 217).
45	- Re-renders the inspector if the renamed box is the one on
46	  display.
47	
48	`002-boxes.js::draw_box` keeps the canvas-rendered port name label
49	(it was briefly removed during the overlay experiment).
50	
51	`assets/js/009-box-overlays.js` and the matching CSS removed; the
52	overlay layer is gone.
53	
54	### Edge cases
55	
56	- **Variadic slots can't be renamed in-inspector** — the field is
57	  disabled. Toggle the group off if you need to break out of the
58	  pattern.
59	- **Renamed port had a wire**: the wire's `to_input` rewrites on
60	  both endpoints; the canvas re-renders with the new label without
61	  losing the connection.
62	- **Typo'd a name to something the function doesn't expect**: this
63	  is the intentional tradeoff from issue 208. The runtime error
64	  surfaces clearly rather than silently skipping.
65	
66	## Current behavior
67	
68	Input port names are read-only labels in the inspector — derived from
69	the function's parameter names when the file browser populates the
70	box (issue 207, 208). Each port row in the inspector also carries a
71	literal value input. With variadic inputs (issue 217), a box with
72	several slots produces a tall, narrow list of name+type+value rows
73	that crowds the sidebar.
74	
75	The canvas already renders port names next to their dots, so the
76	name appears twice — once on the canvas (read-only label) and once
77	in the inspector (read-only label).
78	
79	## Intended behavior
80	
81	Move both the port name and the literal value input out of the
82	inspector and onto the canvas next to the port dot. The inspector
83	keeps only the variadic toggle / remove-slot buttons and any
84	non-port box-level fields (ref / fn / comparator).
85	
86	### Why
87	
88	- The inspector is crowded with redundant data (the canvas already
89	  shows port names).
90	- Literal values often need more horizontal room than a 72px input
91	  field allows, especially for prompts, paths, or JSON snippets.
92	- Reading the box's full configuration without clicking through the
93	  inspector becomes possible — every input's name and value is
94	  visible directly on the canvas.
95	- 208's "read-only port names" decision was about preventing typo
96	  drift between source code and box JSON; the file browser still
97	  populates names from the parsed signature, but the user should be
98	  free to override them after the fact.
99	
100	### Reverses 208's read-only-name decision
101	
102	Issue 208 made input port names non-editable on the principle that
103	they should match the parsed function signature exactly. This issue
104	flips that — names become editable again, with the trade-off that a
105	typo can desynchronize the editor's record from the function the box
106	calls. The cost is a per-box runtime error rather than a hidden
107	silent skip, so it surfaces clearly. The benefit is that users can
108	rename inputs freely (renaming a Lua function's parameter and then
109	updating the box does not require re-browsing).
110	
111	### Layout
112	
113	Each input port row on the canvas becomes:
114	
115	```
116	●  text_0    ┌──────────────────┐
117	             │ "hello world"    │
118	             └──────────────────┘
119	```
120	
121	- The port dot stays on the box's left edge (unchanged).
122	- The port name is an editable text field, rendered as an HTML
123	  overlay positioned next to the dot. Pan and zoom adjust the
124	  overlay's transform to match the canvas.
125	- The literal value field is another HTML overlay below or to the
126	  right of the name.
127	
128	### HTML overlays over the canvas
129	
130	Canvas 2D doesn't do native text inputs, so the editable fields are
131	DOM `<input>` elements absolutely positioned over the canvas. A
132	small per-box overlay container holds them, transforms with the
133	camera (CSS transform applied on every render frame), and updates
134	the underlying `box.inputs[i]` on input.
135	
136	This is the same pattern as the source viewer (issue 215) — DOM
137	elements layered over the canvas, with positions computed from
138	`Canvas.world_to_screen`. The complexity is keeping overlay state
139	in sync with the canvas: they need to be created when a box is
140	loaded, destroyed when a box is deleted, repositioned every frame
141	(or when pan/zoom changes), and hidden when off-screen.
142	
143	### Inspector becomes lighter
144	
145	After this change the inspector shows:
146	- Box label (editable)
147	- ref / fn (with browse / view buttons)
148	- For each input: the variadic toggle (`var` / `var ×` / `×`) only.
149	  No name field, no value field — those live on the canvas.
150	- Output: the comparator toggle and comparand input (unchanged).
151	- Delete-box button (unchanged).
152	
153	Roughly half the height the current inspector takes for variadic
154	boxes.
155	
156	## Open questions
157	
158	- **Overlay performance**: dozens of overlays multiplied by frequent
159	  pan/zoom updates is a real cost. Tests are needed; a bounding-box
160	  cull (only render overlays for visible boxes) is the obvious
161	  optimization if it matters.
162	- **Value-input width**: the field grows with the input's content, or
163	  is a fixed width with horizontal scroll? Probably grow-on-content
164	  with a max width clamp, similar to what monospace text does.
165	- **Editing port names while wires are connected**: renaming a port
166	  while a wire is attached should rename the wire's `to_input` /
167	  `from_branch` on both endpoints atomically — same bilateral-update
168	  pattern the variadic ops use.
169	- **Name validation**: what's a valid port name? Probably `[A-Za-z_][\w]*`
170	  (a Lua / C / shell-compatible identifier). Reject names that start
171	  with digits, contain spaces, etc. Reject duplicates within a box.
172	- **Backward compat**: existing maps with the current layout work as
173	  before — no migration needed. The change is purely UI.
174	
175	## Suggested implementation sequence
176	
177	1. Create `assets/js/009-box-overlays.js` (new module) responsible
178	   for per-box DOM overlays. Public API: `update_overlays_for(box_id)`
179	   to create / refresh overlays for a box, `remove_overlays_for(box_id)`
180	   on box deletion, `redraw_overlays()` on pan/zoom.
181	2. Wire the overlays into the render loop: after `Boxes.draw_all`,
182	   call `redraw_overlays()` to reposition the overlays for the
183	   current camera. The overlays themselves don't redraw — only their
184	   `style.left` / `style.top` / `style.transform` updates.
185	3. Remove the name + value rendering from `mk_port_display` in
186	   `004-inspector.js`. Leave only the variadic controls.
187	4. Remove the canvas-side port name labels from `002-boxes.js::draw_box`
188	   (the overlay's editable input replaces it).
189	5. Wire validation: name must be a valid identifier and unique
190	   within the box's `inputs`. Validate in the input's `oninput`
191	   handler; revert to the previous valid name on rejection.
192	6. Test against `maps/hello`, `maps/classify-demo`, and a fresh map
193	   with variadic inputs (e.g. via `libs/text.lua`'s concat).
194	
195	## Relevant files
196	
197	- `assets/js/009-box-overlays.js` — new module (to be created)
198	- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — port name labels move out
199	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — `mk_port_display` simplified
200	- `assets/js/001-canvas.js` — pan/zoom triggers overlay reposition
... [truncated; 200-line cap]

```
1	# 212 — Editor interaction modes
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	complete
6	
7	## Current behavior
8	
9	Creating a box requires a double-click on the canvas. There is no dedicated
10	wire-erase tool; wires must be clicked individually to select them and then
11	deleted with the keyboard. Deleting a box that has incoming connections fails
12	with a server 409 error because the server checks for references before allowing
13	the delete.
14	
15	## Intended behavior
16	
17	Two tool-mode buttons appear in the top toolbar:
18	
19	**+ box**: clicking the button enters add-box mode (button highlights). The
20	next click on the canvas places a new box at the cursor position and returns
21	to select mode automatically.
22	
23	**✕ wire**: clicking the button enters erase-wire mode (button highlights).
24	While the mouse button is held down and the cursor is dragged across the canvas,
25	any wire the cursor passes over is immediately erased. Releasing the mouse button
26	exits erase-wire mode and returns to select mode.
27	
28	Double-click to create a box is removed; the + box tool is the only creation
29	path.
30	
31	Box deletion is unconditional: before calling the server DELETE, the client
32	strips all connection records referencing the box from every other box (PUT each
33	affected box), then issues the DELETE. The server's 409 reference guard is no
34	longer triggered. No confirmation dialog is shown.
35	
36	## Implementation notes
37	
38	The shipped behavior diverges from the original spec in one place:
39	
40	- **Box creation** is via the canvas right-click context menu ("add
41	  box here") rather than a dedicated `+ box` toolbar mode. One click
42	  instead of two, and consistent with how other context-driven
43	  actions in the editor work.
44	- **Wire erasure** is the dedicated `✕ wire` toolbar tool as
45	  originally specced — click the button, hold-and-drag across the
46	  canvas, wires under the cursor are erased.
47	- `tool_mode` has values `'select' | 'erase-wire'` (no `'add-box'`).
48	- **Box deletion** strips connection references from every other box
49	  client-side before issuing the server `DELETE`, avoiding the 409
50	  reference-guard error.
51	
52	`set_tool_mode(mode)` (in `005-app.js`) manages the active button
53	class and canvas cursor (`'cell'` for erase-wire). Erase-wire tracks
54	a Set of already-queued wire signatures per drag to avoid
55	double-deletes. Connections are removed from the local Boxes cache
56	immediately (for instant visual feedback) and then deleted from the
57	server asynchronously.
58	
59	## Suggested implementation steps
60	
61	1. `005-app.js` — add `tool_mode`, `set_tool_mode`, `wire_sig` helpers.
62	   Update mousedown/mousemove/mouseup handlers for new modes.
63	   Rewrite `delete_box` to strip references before deleting.
64	   Remove dblclick handler.
65	2. `index.html` — add `#tool-add-box` and `#tool-erase-wire` buttons to toolbar.
66	   Add `.toolbar-btn.active` CSS.
67	
68	## Related documents
69	
70	- assets/js/005-app.js — App closure, event handlers
71	- assets/index.html — toolbar HTML and CSS
72	
```

1	# 217 — Concat box with per-input variadic ports and auto-grow/shrink
2	
3	## Status
4	complete
5	
6	## Implementation notes
7	
8	**Part A** — `libs/text.lua` ships `M.concat(sep, ...)` and
9	`M.split(text, sep)`. Concat is variadic to match the editor's
10	variadic-input model. Split returns a Lua array; the driver shim
11	JSON-encodes for the single output wire. The accompanying
12	`libs/text.lua.info.md` documents both functions.
13	
14	**Part B** — Variadic UI lives in `assets/js/004-inspector.js`:
15	
16	- Helpers (`parse_variadic_name`, `is_variadic_slot`,
17	  `variadic_slots_for`, `last_variadic_index`) parse and query the
18	  `<base>_<index>` naming convention for variadic slots.
19	- `make_variadic(port_name)` toggles a non-variadic port into a
20	  one-slot variadic group (rename `text` → `text_0`, add base to
21	  `box.variadic_inputs`, update connection records on this box and
22	  every upstream box).
23	- `unmake_variadic(base)` collapses a group back to a single port
24	  (drop higher slots and their wires, rename slot 0 back to base).
25	- `add_variadic_slot(base)` appends `<base>_<last+1>`. Used by the
26	  auto-grow path; also reachable directly when needed.
27	- `remove_variadic_slot(slot_name)` drops a single slot and renames
28	  higher slots down by one, updating connections atomically per
29	  slot.
30	- `auto_grow_after_set(box, slot_name)` is the public-from-Inspector
31	  helper. If `slot_name` is the last slot of its variadic group, it
32	  appends a new empty slot. Idempotent — calling again on the same
33	  slot is a no-op once the slot is no longer last.
34	
35	The wire-create path (`006-wires.js::create_connection`) calls
36	`Inspector.auto_grow_after_set(dst_box, to_input)` after a
37	successful PUT. The inspector's per-port value-input handler also
38	calls it when a non-empty literal value is set on a slot. Together
39	these cover both growth triggers — wire-connect and value-set.
40	
41	Per-port UI (`mk_port_display`):
42	
43	- Plain port → `var` toggle (turn variadic on).
44	- First slot of a variadic group → `var ×` toggle (collapse back).
45	- Non-first variadic slot → `×` (remove this slot only; higher
46	  slots rename down).
47	- No `+` button — slots auto-grow. Wire-disconnect and
48	  value-clear do NOT shrink (only `×` removes structure).
49	
50	Schema accepts `variadic_inputs` as an optional array of strings
51	(`src/001-schema.lua`).
52	
53	Comparator toggle (`004-inspector.js::cmp_btn.onclick`) now severs
54	all outgoing wires on either toggle direction, fixing the prior
55	behavior where lt/eq/gt wires from a previous comparator state
56	silently re-appeared when the user re-enabled it.
57	
58	## Deliberate omissions / follow-ups
59	
60	- **Auto-shrink on wire-disconnect** is intentionally not done —
61	  the user explicitly chose "explicit `×` is the only way to shrink"
62	  so transient editing doesn't lose structure.
63	- **Editable port names** and **value editing on the canvas
64	  itself** belong in issue 224 — bigger redesign than 217 covers.
65	- **Future ideas** (language-spec-driven variadic detection,
66	  alternative variadic shapes for keyword args / streams /
67	  discriminated unions) are captured below.
68	
69	## Current behavior
70	No standard concat box exists. No mechanism exists for a box to have a variable
71	number of input wires on a single logical port.
72	
73	## Intended behavior
74	
75	### Part A — text library box
76	`libs/text.lua` exposes two functions:
77	
78	`M.concat(sep, ...)` — join N strings:
79	- `sep` — separator string (e.g. `"\n\n"`)
80	- `...` — any number of text values to join (variadic)
81	- returns a single `result` port
82	
83	The function is variadic so its `text` input can expand to any count without
84	modifying the source file.
85	
86	`M.split(text, sep)` — split one string into parts:
87	- `text` — the input string
88	- `sep` — the separator to split on (plain string, not a pattern)
89	- returns a single `result` port containing a JSON-encoded array of the parts
90	
91	Output ports are named at wire-design time, so a variable number of runtime
92	parts cannot map to separate wires. A JSON array on one port is the only
93	consistent design — the downstream box decodes it.
94	
95	Note: one output wire per box is a deliberate design constraint, not a missing
96	feature. Positional multi-output was explicitly rejected as fragile (multiple
97	return statements with the same tuple width are indistinguishable). The
98	comparator (lt/eq/gt branching) is the only multi-path mechanism. Whatever the
99	function returns goes down the single output wire as one value — arrays or JSON
100	objects if multiple values need to travel together.
101	
102	### Part B — per-input variadic flag
103	
104	Variadic is a property of a specific input port, not the box as a whole. A box
105	could have `sep` (fixed) and `text` (variadic). The inspector shows a toggle on
106	each input to mark it variadic.
107	
108	#### Data model
109	
110	The box JSON gains a `variadic_inputs` set (array of base names):
111	```json
112	{
113	  "inputs": ["sep", "text_0", "text_1", "text_2"],
114	  "variadic_inputs": ["text"]
115	}
116	```
117	
118	Slots of a variadic input are stored as `<base>_<index>` in the `inputs` array.
119	The executor already passes all inputs positionally — no executor changes needed.
120	Connections reference slots by their indexed name (`"text_0"`, `"text_1"`, etc.).
121	
122	The parser (Lua: detects `...`, bash: detects `$@` after shift) can auto-populate
123	`variadic_inputs` when a file is selected via the file browser. For other languages
124	(C, etc.) the user sets the toggle manually in the inspector — the executor contract
125	already supports N positional args via `arg_count`, so C functions reading `argv[]`
126	in a loop work without any changes.
127	
128	#### Auto-grow
129	When a wire is connected to the LAST slot of a variadic input, the editor appends
130	a new empty slot (`text_N+1`) to `box.inputs` and saves the box. The new slot
131	appears immediately as an open port.
132	
133	#### Auto-shrink
134	When a wire is disconnected from any slot of a variadic input:
135	1. Remove that slot from `box.inputs`.
136	2. Rename all subsequent slots down by one (`text_2` → `text_1`, etc.).
137	3. Update every connection that referenced a renamed slot:
138	   - `to_input` in the connection record on this box
139	   - The corresponding side on the source box (connections are stored in both
140	     endpoint files; both must be updated atomically).
141	4. Keep at least one slot for the variadic input (never shrink below `text_0`).
142	
143	Middle-port removal is handled by the same rename-and-compact step: remove the cut
144	slot, shift all higher-indexed slots down, update their wires. The user always sees
145	a dense, gap-free list of ports.
146	
147	## Open questions before implementing Part B
148	- Rename atomicity: the server's `PUT /maps/:name/boxes/:id` writes one box at a
149	  time. Updating the connection in the source box requires a second PUT. These are
150	  two separate writes — consider whether a partial failure leaves the map in an
151	  inconsistent state, and whether a `PUT /maps/:name/connections` batch endpoint
152	  is worth adding first.
153	- Should the file browser parser set `variadic_inputs` automatically when it
154	  detects `...` in a Lua signature, and if so, which argument specifically?
155	
156	  -- nah let's just make the user set the variadic property manually. Unless we can put it in the language driver
157	     somehow? but I don't think that's really possible in the design. Thoughts?
158	
159	## Suggested implementation sequence
160	1. Part A: implement `libs/text.lua` with `M.concat(sep, ...)`.
161	2. Part B schema: add `variadic_inputs` to box schema and validator.
162	3. Part B editor: auto-grow on wire connect; auto-shrink + rename on wire disconnect.
163	4. Part B inspector: variadic toggle per input port.
164	5. Part B parser: auto-detect `...` in Lua and set `variadic_inputs`.
165	
166	## Future development ideas
167	
168	### Language-spec-driven variadic detection
169	
170	Currently the editor uses hard-coded heuristics per language to find
171	variadic indicators in a function signature (Lua: literal `...`, Bash:
172	`$@` after `shift`). The cleaner architecture mirrors the phase 3
173	language-spec model (issue 303): each language declares its own way
174	to recognize and surface variadic parameters, and the file-browser
175	parser asks the language spec rather than embedding the rules.
176	
177	Concretely, the editor side of a language spec
178	(`langs/<name>/lexer.js` or a sibling `parser.js`) could expose:
179	
180	```js
181	// Returns { name, inputs: ["sep", "text"], variadic_inputs: ["text"] }
182	// for `function M.concat(sep, ...)` in Lua, or
183	// `concat() { local sep="$1"; shift; ... "$@" }` in Bash.
184	function parse_function_signature(source, fn_name) { ... }
185	```
186	
187	The editor still calls the same on-select handler; the language spec
188	is responsible for deciding how to translate native variadic syntax
189	into the editor's `(inputs, variadic_inputs)` model.
190	
191	### Variadic shapes that don't map cleanly to an array
192	
193	The current model assumes variadic = an ordered series of positional
194	slots. That fits Lua `...`, C `va_list`, Bash `"$@"`, JavaScript rest
195	params, etc. It does not fit:
196	
197	- **Keyword arguments** (Python `**kwargs`, Ruby keyword splats) —
198	  these are key/value pairs, not positional. A box would need
199	  named-slot pairs the user labels per slot, not auto-numbered
200	  `_0` / `_1` / `_2`.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

## Assistant

Five issues created: 234 (middle-click duplicate), 235 (value replaces port name), 236 (filename header + `fn()` output), 237 (text library expansion), 238 (server config file).

Now — the data box one. You asked for help managing it, so let me lay out what I see and ask back:

**What you already have**: issue 229 (open) proposes splitting data IO into **two boxes** — a `data` source (path → emits file bytes) and a paired `file_write` sink (path + text inputs → writes to disk). It is language-agnostic and lives in the dispatch layer.

**What you described today** sounds like a *third* design: **one box** that is both a read and a write, dispatching on input type — pointer → memory location in the thread pool slot store, string → filepath. Plus a UI slider for the "no input given" case to choose between `/tmp/`-text-file and C-memory-pointer-from-slot-store.

I think you got tangled because three different axes are mixing in one box:

1. **read vs write** — is this thing a source or a sink?
2. **disk vs RAM (slot store)** — where does the data live?
3. **input-driven path vs static path / auto-allocated** — does the user wire in the location, type a literal, or let the box pick?

229 cleanly splits axis 1 (two boxes). Your idea today collapses all three into one box and uses input *type* (pointer vs string) as the disambiguator. That's elegant in one way (one node, fewer concepts) and tangled in another (the box's behavior changes shape based on what is wired in, which is the kind of thing that bites later).

A couple of questions back to you before I write anything:

- **Why a pointer at all in the editor surface?** The slot store (issue 302) already gives every wire a slot ID — that *is* the "C memory location." If a box wants to talk about RAM, the slot is the natural handle, not a raw pointer. Would "save to slot N / read from slot N" feel right, where N is just an integer wire?
- **Is the "/tmp/ vs C memory" slider the same axis as "is the input a path string or a pointer"?** If yes, the slider is redundant when something is wired (the input type already says which). If no, then there are really two switches and the slider is doing something separate — what?
- **Do you want one box that flips modes, or two boxes that each do one thing well?** 229 already chose the second path. Is today's idea a revision of 229, or a different thing that should live alongside it?

If you want, I can either (a) **update 229** to absorb your new thinking (probably re-scope it as "language-agnostic IO primitives — source and sink, with disk and RAM-slot variants"), or (b) **leave 229 alone and write a fresh issue** for the unified read/write data box if that turns out to be a separate concept. Per the project's "build history vertically" rule, I lean toward updating 229 — but I'd rather you tell me which of those three axes you actually care about before I commit either way.

## Assistant

Sure, let me back up.

Issue 236 says **the box header should display the filename of the function the box calls** (its `ref`). E.g. a box pointing at `libs/text.lua::concat` shows `text.lua` in the header.

That rule works fine for `call` boxes — they have a `ref` pointing at a source file. But the editor has other kinds of boxes too:

- `read` and `write` (new, from updated 229) — no ref, they're dispatch-layer primitives
- `branch` — predicate routing, no ref
- `iterator` (issue 221) — loops, no ref
- `comparator` (the lt/eq/gt output mode) — also has no ref of its own

For those, there's no filename to put in the header. So *something else* has to go there by default.

In 236 I left a fallback rule: "if there's no ref, use the kind name." That means a `read` box's header would just say `read`, a `branch` would say `branch`, etc. Functional, but kind of bland — and somebody has to decide whether `read`'s header should say `"read"` or `"read file"` or just an icon, etc.

**That decision — what each kind's default header text is — needs to live somewhere.** It's a one-line-per-kind lookup table:

| kind | default header |
|------|---------------|
| call | (basename of ref) |
| read | "read" |
| write | "write" |
| branch | "branch" |
| iterator | "iter" |
| comparator | "?" |

My question was: do you want me to **pick those default strings now** and write them into 236 (or into a new tiny issue), or **leave it open** for whoever implements 236 to pick on the fly?

Either is fine — I just wanted to flag that the table doesn't exist yet and somebody will have to fill it in.

1	// Wire rendering, hit testing, and connection management.
2	// Wires are derived from box connection data — no separate wire store.

4	const Wires = (() => {
5	  const BEZIER_CTRL_OFFSET = 80;
6	  // LOOP_OFFSET pulls a self-loop's control points further out and
7	  // below the box body, so the curve sweeps around the bottom rather
8	  // than cutting straight through the rectangle (issue 228).
9	  const LOOP_OFFSET        = 80;
10	  const HIT_SAMPLES        = 20;
11	  const HIT_THRESHOLD_PX   = 8;
12	
13	  // Colors for comparator branch wires; plain wire uses default
14	  const BRANCH_COLOR = { lt: '#ff8c42', eq: '#4a9eff', gt: '#4caf7d' };
15	
16	  let _selected_wire = null;  // { from_box, from_branch, to_box, to_input }
17	
18	  // {{{ selected_wire
19	  function selected_wire() { return _selected_wire; }
20	  // }}}
21	
22	  // {{{ bezier_point
23	  function bezier_point(t, x0, y0, cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1, x1, y1) {
24	    const mt = 1 - t;
25	    return {
26	      x: mt*mt*mt*x0 + 3*mt*mt*t*cx0 + 3*mt*t*t*cx1 + t*t*t*x1,
27	      y: mt*mt*mt*y0 + 3*mt*mt*t*cy0 + 3*mt*t*t*cy1 + t*t*t*y1,
28	    };
29	  }
30	  // }}}
31	
32	  // {{{ endpoint_controls
33	  // Returns the two cubic-Bezier control points for a wire from src to
34	  // dst. For a normal wire the controls jut horizontally out of each
35	  // port. For a self-loop (output and input on the same box body), the
36	  // controls swing out and below so the curve routes around the
37	  // rectangle instead of slicing through it (issue 228).
38	  function endpoint_controls(src, dst, self_loop_box) {
39	    if (self_loop_box) {
40	      const h = Boxes.box_height(self_loop_box);
41	      return {
42	        cx0: src.x + LOOP_OFFSET, cy0: src.y + h * 0.7,
43	        cx1: dst.x - LOOP_OFFSET, cy1: dst.y + h * 0.7,
44	      };
45	    }
46	    return {
47	      cx0: src.x + BEZIER_CTRL_OFFSET, cy0: src.y,
48	      cx1: dst.x - BEZIER_CTRL_OFFSET, cy1: dst.y,
49	    };
50	  }
51	  // }}}
52	
53	  // {{{ draw_bezier_cp
54	  // Draws a cubic Bezier with explicit control points. The wrapper
55	  // draw_bezier (kept for the in-progress wire-drag preview) calls
56	  // this with the default straight-out controls.
57	  function draw_bezier_cp(ctx, x0, y0, cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1, x1, y1, color, selected) {
58	    ctx.beginPath();
59	    ctx.moveTo(x0, y0);
60	    ctx.bezierCurveTo(cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1, x1, y1);
61	    ctx.strokeStyle = selected ? '#ffffff' : color;
62	    ctx.lineWidth   = selected ? 2.5 : 1.5;
63	    ctx.setLineDash([]);
64	    ctx.stroke();
65	  }
66	  // }}}
67	
68	  // {{{ draw_bezier
69	  function draw_bezier(ctx, x0, y0, x1, y1, color, selected) {
70	    const cx0 = x0 + BEZIER_CTRL_OFFSET;
71	    const cy0 = y0;
72	    const cx1 = x1 - BEZIER_CTRL_OFFSET;
73	    const cy1 = y1;
74	    draw_bezier_cp(ctx, x0, y0, cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1, x1, y1, color, selected);
75	  }
76	  // }}}
77	
78	  // {{{ hit_test_wire
79	  // Returns the connection record if cursor (wx,wy) is within HIT_THRESHOLD pixels
80	  // of any wire, or null. Operates in world space; threshold is adjusted for zoom.
81	  function hit_test_wire(wx, wy, zoom) {
82	    const threshold = HIT_THRESHOLD_PX / zoom;
83	    for (const id in Boxes.boxes) {
84	      const box = Boxes.boxes[id];
85	      for (const c of box.connections || []) {
86	        if (c.from_box !== id) continue;
87	
88	        const src  = Boxes.get_port_world_pos(c.from_box, c.from_branch ?? null, 'output');
89	        const dst  = Boxes.get_port_world_pos(c.to_box,   c.to_input,            'input');
90	        if (!src || !dst) continue;
91	
92	        // Self-loop wires use the looped control points, so sample
93	        // along that same curve — clicks register on the visual wire
94	        // path rather than on a phantom straight-through path.
95	        const self_box = (c.from_box === c.to_box) ? Boxes.boxes[c.from_box] : null;
96	        const { cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1 } = endpoint_controls(src, dst, self_box);
97	
98	        for (let i = 0; i <= HIT_SAMPLES; i++) {
99	          const t = i / HIT_SAMPLES;
100	          const p = bezier_point(t, src.x, src.y, cx0, cy0, cx1, cy1, dst.x, dst.y);
101	          const dx = wx - p.x, dy = wy - p.y;
102	          if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= threshold*threshold) return c;
103	        }
104	      }

28	  // Issue 235 surfaces this by deciding the canvas label — port name
29	  // when wired (so the reader doesn't mistake the dormant value for

42	  //   - wire attached, or no value → port name (parameter identity)
43	  //   - value set, no wire         → the value (what the box does)
44	  //   - value too long / multiline → first line, truncated with …
45	  // The two-branch decision is the visible heart of issue 235: a
46	  // reader scanning the map should see the data the box operates on,
47	  // not the parameter slot it sits in.
48	  function port_label_text(box, i) {
49	    const p = box.inputs[i];

1	// Canvas viewport: pan + zoom, and the per-frame draw transform.
2	//
3	// Coordinate model
4	//   screen pixel (sx, sy)  ←→  world point (wx, wy)
5	//     sx = wx * zoom + pan_x
6	//     wx = (sx - pan_x) / zoom
7	//
8	// `pan_x` / `pan_y` are screen-pixel offsets passed to ctx.translate
9	// AFTER scaling: the world is scaled around its origin first, then the
10	// result is shifted on screen. This is simpler than a world-space camera
11	// because every operation is either pure-screen-pixel arithmetic
12	// (panning, mouse positions) or pure-world arithmetic (drawing). They
13	// only meet in two functions: world_to_screen and screen_to_world.
14	
15	const Canvas = (() => {
16	  const el  = document.getElementById('canvas');
17	  const ctx = el.getContext('2d');
18	
19	  let zoom  = 1;
20	  let pan_x = 0;
21	  let pan_y = 0;
22	
23	  let dirty = true;
24	
25	  // {{{ resize
26	  function resize() {
27	    el.width  = window.innerWidth  - (document.getElementById('sidebar').offsetWidth || 0);
28	    el.height = window.innerHeight;
29	    dirty = true;
30	  }
31	  // }}}
32	
33	  // {{{ world_to_screen
34	  function world_to_screen(wx, wy) {
35	    return { x: wx * zoom + pan_x, y: wy * zoom + pan_y };
36	  }
37	  // }}}
38	
39	  // {{{ screen_to_world
40	  function screen_to_world(sx, sy) {
41	    return { x: (sx - pan_x) / zoom, y: (sy - pan_y) / zoom };
42	  }
43	  // }}}
44	
45	  // {{{ mark_dirty
46	  function mark_dirty() { dirty = true; }
47	  // }}}
48	
49	  // {{{ start_frame
50	  // Reset the transform, clear, then set up the world-drawing transform.

**Tooltips**: they only appear when the canvas label had to be shortened — i.e. the value is longer than 24 chars or contains a real newline. Your test cases (`5`, etc.) were short enough to render in full, so no tooltip was warranted. If you set a port value to something like `"this is a very long literal that won't fit in the box"` and hover the port dot, you should see the full text float near the cursor.

**"Hovering on a port should hide it?"**: my fault, that was a confusingly worded sentence. I meant *mouseleave on the whole canvas* (cursor exits the canvas area entirely) clears any showing tooltip — pure housekeeping so a stale tooltip doesn't linger. Hovering a port does NOT hide anything.

**Editable title overrides filename**: yes, 236 already specs exactly that — `box.label` overrides the filename default. Good, no change needed.

## Assistant

Committed (commit `2ce3f72`). Only my 10 files; the other dev's working-tree changes are untouched.

Now the script-location question — a canonical map of every place runnable / loadable code lives in this project:

| Location | What it holds | Who runs it |
|---|---|---|
| `scripts/` | Top-level operator scripts: `start-server.sh`, `run-tests.sh`, `create-map.sh`, `open-editor.sh`, `soramech-compile.sh`, `test-server.sh` | You, from a shell |
| `demo.sh`, `run` (root) | Phase-demo entry points | You, from a shell |
| `src/` | Editor server + executor + schema/loader in Lua (`001-005-*.lua`), plus phase-3 C runtime (`008-014-*.c`) | The server / pool runtime |
| `assets/js/` | Editor frontend modules (`001-canvas.js` through `008-source-view.js`) | The browser |
| `drivers/` | Per-language driver shims (`bash.sh`, `c.sh`, `lua.sh`) that bridge a box invocation to its language | The executor |
| `langs/<lang>/` | Phase-3 language-spec C glue and per-language build (`spec.c`, `Makefile`, e.g. `langs/bash/bash-server.sh`) | The dispatch layer |
| `libs/` | Bundled "library box" modules every map can `ref` directly: `text.lua`, `files.lua`, `ollama.lua`, `soramech-data.lua`, `dkjson.lua`, plus the C sub-libs `json/` and `task-pool/` | Boxes via `ref` |
| `maps/<map>/src/` | Per-map user scripts — the actual functions called by each `call` box in that map | The executor when running that map |
| `tests/maps/<map>/src/` | Same idea but inside the test-map fixtures | The C test binaries |
| `tests/` | C test binaries and test runners | `scripts/run-tests.sh` |

If you're looking for something specific and can't find it, the first two places to check are `libs/` (bundled, project-wide) and `maps/<your-map>/src/` (per-map). Everything else is infrastructure.

Ready to start on 236 — say go when you've decided.

## User

the files that we intended to run in the call boxes. where are we storing those? I don't need to know about source code related to sora itself, only the tools in the toolbox we're preparing to give to the user.

## Assistant

Two places, by scope:

- **`libs/`** — bundled, shared across every map. Currently: `text.lua`, `files.lua`, `ollama.lua`, `soramech-data.lua`, `dkjson.lua`. Any map's call box can `ref` these directly.
- **`maps/<map>/src/`** — scoped to one map. Scripts only that map's boxes can ref.

That's the whole toolbox surface. Issue 238 (the editor-server config file) is the one that would let a user add their own bundled-default directory alongside `libs/`, so they could keep their personal toolbox somewhere outside this repo and still have it show up in the file browser.

## Assistant

No, and that's worth flagging — `libs/` is **not** pre-selected by default. Here's what `scripts/create-map.sh` actually does today:

A new map's `meta.json` seeds `src_dirs` with just **the map's own `src/` directory**. The file browser only lists what's in `src_dirs`. So `libs/` won't appear in a freshly-created map until the user adds it via the file-browser's dir-picker (or edits `meta.json` by hand).

There's a partial-truth-counterpart though: at runtime, the server's `package.path` includes `${DIR}/libs/?.lua`, so a function that does `require("text")` resolves to `libs/text.lua` whether or not the editor knows about it. That means `libs/` is **available for Lua to load**, just **not browseable in the editor by default**.

That gap is exactly what issue 238 is set up to fix — the config file would carry a `bundled_script_dirs` list (defaulting to `[libs/]`) that `create-map.sh` (or its replacement) drops straight into every new map's `src_dirs`. After 238 lands, "every new map sees `libs/` in the file browser without the user lifting a finger" would be the default.

Want me to:

- **(a)** add a small note to 238 making the create-map auto-population explicit (it's implied but not spelled out), or
- **(b)** open it as a separate tiny issue ("create-map.sh seeds bundled dirs from server config") so the two changes can land independently, or
- **(c)** leave 238 as-is and treat the auto-population as obvious-on-implementation?

## Assistant

Yes — user-added dirs (the ones you'd add via the `+ library dir` button) are treated identically. They live in `meta.src_dirs` alongside the map's own `src/`, so on the server they're tagged `kind=map`, and in the file browser they get the same green `bundled` tag and the same right-click → hide menu. Hiding one of them just removes its entry from `meta.src_dirs`.

So the full picture in the browser right now is:

| dir origin | where it's stored | tag | hide action |
|---|---|---|---|
| map's own `src/` | `meta.src_dirs` (or implicit fallback) | bundled | drop from `src_dirs` |
| `+ library dir` (per-map add) | `meta.src_dirs` | bundled | drop from `src_dirs` |
| editor config `bundled_script_dirs` | `config/editor-server.lua` | bundled | add to `meta.hidden_bundled_dirs` |

Goal: replace the Lua synchronous runner with a C thread pool runner
that executes box graphs in parallel.

The phase is fully designed; implementation is now underway.
Issues 301–313 are the architectural specification. As each issue
moves into implementation, its row is marked "in progress"; on
completion it's moved to `issues/completed/`.

| 303 | language runtime spec (pluggable per-language invocation)   | in progress · registry + per-worker init + pool hook + lang filtering |
| 304 | task dispatch layer (C, replaces synchronous executor)      | in progress · plain + comparator + iterator + randomizer + weighted routing + cell-tagged iterator ordering |
| 305 | C graph loader (replaces `003-loader.lua`)                  | in progress · attach + lang enum + 312 fast-path classification |
| 306 | Lua language spec implementation                            | in progress · init + invoke + teardown + per-worker module cache done |

| 311 | integration tests & run output (`last-run.jsonl`)           | in progress · writer + queue + thread + LOG_VALUES + LOG_SLOTS + run-tests.sh + compile-pipeline check |

| 313 | research: whole-program same-language merge                 | research / blocked |

==================== 230
# 230 — Required inputs, the optional flag, and no-null-from-missing

## Status
open

## Current behavior

A box's input port can be left without a wire and without a literal
value. The phase 2 executor runs the box anyway; the function on
the other end gets `nil` (Lua), unset (Bash), or whatever default
the language hands a missing positional arg. Errors only surface if
the function happens to reference the missing arg in a way the
language can't tolerate (e.g. `string.upper(nil)` in Lua).

This is a class of silent-error bug. The user had a wiring intent,
...
## Intended behavior

Every input port is in one of three states by **compile time**:

1. A wire is connected to it.
2. A literal value is set on it.
3. It carries `optional: true` on the port record.
==================== 231
# 231 — Move per-language signature parsers out of the file browser into `langs/<name>/parser.js`

## Status
open

## Current behavior

`assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` carries language-specific code:
- `parse_lua(content)` — extracts `function M.<name>(<params>)`
  declarations and scans for `return` statements to enumerate
  outputs.
- `parse_bash(content)` — extracts `<name>() {` declarations and
  uses `parse_bash_inputs` to count `$N` references.
- `parse_functions(content, filename)` — dispatches on file
  extension to one of the above.
...
## Intended behavior

Each language directory carries a `parser.js` ES module exporting
a single function:

```js
// langs/<name>/parser.js
==================== 233
# 233 — Unified `routing` schema for branching boxes

## Status
open

## Concept

A "branching box" is a regular call box whose output, after the
function runs, is sent to **one of several** downstream
destinations rather than to all of them. Same function, same
inputs, same output value — only the routing decision differs.
Currently we have two flavors:

- **Comparator**: routes the function's output by comparing it
  to a stored threshold. Three fixed branches (`lt` / `eq` /
...
## Intended behavior

Every call box carries a `routing` field. No implicit defaulting:
plain boxes have `routing: { "kind": "plain" }`, the same way
branched boxes carry their own kind. Explicit and consistent —
no "what if I forget the routing field" surprises at validation
time.
==================== 315
# 315 — Reference-counted compiled-map artifacts

## Status
open

## Current behavior

`scripts/soramech-compile.sh` from issue 309 is destructive at the
top:

```bash
rm -rf "$COMPILED"
mkdir -p "$COMPILED" "$COMPILED/boxes" "$COMPILED/src" ...
```

...
## Intended behavior

A compiled artifact carries its own reference count. Other
programs that depend on a particular build of a map are expected
to *acquire* a reference on startup and *release* it on shutdown.
When `soramech-compile` is asked to rebuild a map that has any
live references, it does NOT clobber the existing directory.
==================== 316
# 316 — Simple test runner script

## Status
open

## Current behavior

`make test` builds and runs every unit-test binary plus the
integration runner. The output is helpful when you want to verify
that everything still passes at the end of a working session, but
it's noisy when you're iterating on one specific change:

- The build phase prints `→ src/foo.c`, `→ src/bar.c`, ... for
  every file that compiles or relinks. On a clean tree that's
  dozens of lines before the first test runs.
...
## Intended behavior

A separate script (`scripts/run-unit-tests.sh`) that runs the
already-built unit-test binaries one by one and reports their
results in a uniform format. No build step. No make. Just:
locate the binaries, run them in turn, capture stdout/stderr,
print a one-line per-binary summary, and at the end print a
==================== 317
# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes

## Status
open

## Current behavior

A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
table as a data box's value, you either:

- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
...
## Intended behavior

Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):

```c
/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
```

## Assistant

Here's the unstarted set, grouped by area:

**Editor / phase-2 extensions** — fully unstarted, ready for pickup:

| ID | Title | One-line summary |
|---|---|---|
| **229** | `read` / `write` box kinds (language-agnostic IO) | Three box kinds — `call` (existing), `read` (path→contents), `write` (path+value→done). Optional outputs are discarded when no wire. Bigger scope: touches schema, loader, dispatch (phase-3 territory). |
| **230** | Required inputs and the optional flag | Every input must have a wire, a literal, or `optional: true` at compile time. Catches silent-nil bugs where a missing wire produces unset args at runtime. |
| **231** | Move signature parsers into `langs/<name>/parser.js` | Extract `parse_lua` / `parse_bash` out of the file browser into per-language ES modules, so adding a new language doesn't require editing the editor. |
| **233** | Unified routing schema for branching boxes | Every call box carries an explicit `routing` field (plain / comparator / iterator / etc.) instead of inferring the kind from sibling fields. Replaces the current ad-hoc comparator+iterator schemas. |
| **234** | Middle-click duplicates the selected box | Short middle-mouse click on the canvas while a box is selected creates a duplicate with the same ref/fn, literal values, and incoming wires. |
| **236** | Box header = filename, output label = `fn()` | Box header shows the basename of `ref`; output port shows `fn_name()` for `call` boxes. Per-kind defaults for non-call boxes. User label still overrides. |
| **237** | String-manipulation library expansion | Grow `libs/text.lua` with upper/lower/trim/replace/replace_first/contains/starts_with/ends_with/length/substring. |

**Phase-3 runtime** — the other dev's territory; mostly already in flight:

- **301–312, 314** all marked "in progress" — pool, slot store, language specs, dispatch, graph loader, build system, etc.
- **313** is research/blocked (whole-program same-language merge)
- **315** — Reference-counted compiled-map artifacts (don't clobber a build that other tools are using)
- **316** — Simple test runner script (run existing test binaries without rebuilding everything)
- **317** — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes (per-language to-JSON / from-JSON callbacks, so a Lua data box can author with native tables)

If you want to keep working in the editor, **234** is the smallest visible win, **236** is the most stylistically impactful (every box's header changes), and **237** is pure additive scripting with no UI risk. **229** is biggest because it crosses into the phase-3 runtime — best deferred until the dispatch layer settles.

1	# text.lua

3	Text manipulation helpers — concat and split. Pure Lua, no external
4	dependencies. Use as `require("text")` from another Lua function, or
5	copy into a map's `src/` and pick the function via the file browser.
6	
7	Both functions output a single value on the box's output wire (per
8	the single-output rule, issue 218).
9	
10	---
11	
12	## M.concat(sep, ...) → string
13	
14	Joins the variadic text arguments with the given separator.
15	
16	| param | type    | description                                          |
17	|-------|---------|------------------------------------------------------|
18	| sep   | string  | separator inserted between every pair of values     |
19	| ...   | strings | any number of text values to join                    |
20	
21	Returns: a single string with all values concatenated.
22	
23	The signature is variadic so the box's `text` input can be marked
24	variadic in the inspector and grown to any number of slots — see
25	issue 217 part B for the editor mechanics.
26	
27	Edge cases: `sep` may be an empty string. Zero-arg call returns the
28	empty string.
29	
30	---
31	
32	## M.split(text, sep) → table
33	
34	Splits `text` into parts on a literal (non-pattern) separator. Returns
35	a Lua array; the driver shim JSON-encodes it for transport on the
36	single output wire. The downstream box decodes the array.
37	
38	| param | type   | description                                          |
39	|-------|--------|------------------------------------------------------|
40	| text  | string | the input string                                     |
41	| sep   | string | the separator to split on (literal — no pattern matching) |
42	
43	Returns: an array (Lua table with integer keys) of the parts.
44	
45	Edge cases:
46	- `nil` text → empty array
47	- `nil` or empty `sep` → single-element array containing the whole input
48	  (avoids an infinite loop and matches common library behavior)
49	- Separator characters that are also Lua-pattern metacharacters (`.`,
50	  `*`, `[`, etc.) are matched literally — `string.find` is called with
51	  `plain = true`.
52

1	-- Text manipulation — shared library and ready-to-use box source.
2	-- require("text") from any Lua function, or copy to a map's src/ to use
3	-- it directly as a call box via the file browser.
4	-- No external dependencies.
5	
6	local M = {}
7	
8	-- {{{ M.concat
9	-- Join N text values with a separator. The signature is variadic so the
10	-- box can grow its `text` input without modifying this source — see
11	-- issue 217 part B for the editor-side variadic-input mechanics.
12	function M.concat(sep, ...)
13	    return table.concat({ ... }, sep or "")
14	end
15	-- }}}
16	
17	-- {{{ M.split
18	-- Split a text into parts on a literal (non-pattern) separator. Returns
19	-- a Lua array of the parts; the driver shim encodes it as JSON for
20	-- transport on the single output wire. The downstream box decodes the
21	-- array — there is no fan-out into multiple wires (that would require
22	-- knowing the part count at design time, which we don't).
23	--
24	-- Plain-string match: the separator is escaped before being passed to
25	-- string.find so users don't have to think about Lua patterns.
26	function M.split(text, sep)
27	    if text == nil then return {} end
28	    if sep == nil or sep == "" then
29	        -- splitting on empty separator: return a single-element array
30	        -- with the whole input. Avoids an infinite loop and matches
31	        -- common library behavior for split-on-empty.
32	        return { text }
33	    end
34	
35	    local parts = {}
36	    local plain = true   -- treat sep as a literal string, not a pattern
37	    local pos   = 1
38	
39	    while true do
40	        local s, e = string.find(text, sep, pos, plain)
41	        if s == nil then
42	            parts[#parts + 1] = string.sub(text, pos)
43	            break
44	        end
45	        parts[#parts + 1] = string.sub(text, pos, s - 1)
46	        pos = e + 1
47	    end
48	
49	    return parts
50	end
51	-- }}}
52	
53	return M
54

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Lua/LuaJIT language driver for SoraMech.
# Loads a .lua module, calls the named function with JSON-decoded arguments,
# and writes the single return value as a JSON value to stdout.
# Contract: <script> <file-path> <fn-name> <arg-count> [<arg> ...]

DIR="/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech"

FILE_PATH="${1}"
FN_NAME="${2}"
ARG_COUNT="${3}"
shift 3

if [ -z "${FILE_PATH}" ] || [ -z "${FN_NAME}" ]; then
    echo "lua.sh: missing file-path or fn-name" >&2
    exit 1
fi

if ! command -v luajit > /dev/null 2>&1; then
    echo "lua.sh: luajit not found on PATH" >&2
    exit 1
fi

if [ ! -f "${FILE_PATH}" ]; then
    echo "lua.sh: file not found: ${FILE_PATH}" >&2
    exit 1
fi

# build a JSON array of the raw argument strings for passing into lua
ARGS_JSON="["
SEP=""
for ARG in "$@"; do
    ARGS_JSON="${ARGS_JSON}${SEP}${ARG}"
    SEP=","
done
ARGS_JSON="${ARGS_JSON}]"

# set LUA_PATH so dkjson is findable before the shim runs its first require
export LUA_PATH="${DIR}/libs/?.lua;${LUA_PATH:-;}"

# run luajit with the driver shim inline; the shim loads the module,
# decodes arguments, calls the function, and encodes the result
luajit - "${FILE_PATH}" "${FN_NAME}" "${ARGS_JSON}" << 'LUASHIM'
local json = require("dkjson")

-- read driver arguments
local file_path = arg[1]
local fn_name   = arg[2]
local args_raw  = arg[3]
```

```
-- add libs and the module's own directory to the path for any further requires
local script_dir = file_path:match("^(.+)/[^/]+$") or "."
package.path = script_dir .. "/?.lua;" ..
               "/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/libs/?.lua;" ..
               package.path

-- expose map root as a global so box functions can access data files without
-- needing it wired as an explicit input; convention: src/ is one level below root
SORAMECH_MAP_DIR = script_dir:match("^(.+)/[^/]+$") or script_dir

-- decode args array
local args_table, _, jerr = json.decode(args_raw)
if not args_table then
    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: failed to decode args JSON: " .. tostring(jerr) .. "\n")
    os.exit(1)
end

-- load the module
local ok, mod = pcall(dofile, file_path)
if not ok then
    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: error loading " .. file_path .. ": " .. tostring(mod) .. "\n")
    os.exit(1)
end

-- resolve the function
local fn = mod[fn_name]
if type(fn) ~= "function" then
    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: no public function '" .. fn_name .. "' in " .. file_path .. "\n")
    os.exit(1)
end

-- decode each arg from JSON to lua value; strings are passed as-is
local call_args = {}
for i, raw_arg in ipairs(args_table) do
    local val, _, aerr = json.decode(raw_arg)
    if aerr then
        -- not valid JSON — treat as a plain string
        call_args[i] = raw_arg
    else
        call_args[i] = val
```

LUASHIM

## Assistant

Here's where we stand:

**Code today**: `data` is recognized by the schema as a valid `kind` (alongside `call`) and offered in the inspector's kind dropdown, but it has **no distinct runtime semantics** — the executor treats it like a `call` box. The only difference is the green badge color on the canvas. Functionally cosmetic.

**Issue 229 (open)**: I redesigned the spec earlier to replace `data` with two dispatch-layer primitives — `read` (path → contents) and `write` (path + value → done signal). Neither is implemented yet. The design hasn't included a "literal output value" mode.

**What you're proposing** is a third mode I hadn't captured in 229: the data box itself can carry an inline literal output value. Logic:

- If a literal `output_value` is set in the inspector → emit that directly, no I/O.
- Else if a `path` input is supplied (wire or literal) → read the file at path and emit its contents.
- Else → error.

This is the same precedence rule input ports already follow (wire > literal value), just applied to the output side. Same mental model.

This is a nicer design than 229's current "two separate kinds" approach in one important way: a user who just wants to drop a small inline config value into a graph (a port, a prompt, a constant) doesn't need a real file at all — they type it into the inspector and that's the box. If they later want to externalize it, they can swap the literal for a `path` input pointing at a file.

**Decision point**: do you want me to **redesign 229** around this single-`data`-kind-with-literal-or-file behavior (keeping `write` as a separate sink kind, since it really is a different concern), or **leave 229 as-is and open a new issue** for "literal output on data boxes" as a smaller, separately-implementable feature on top?

My recommendation: update 229 in place. The literal-output idea subsumes the `read` half cleanly, and you've already once-redesigned 229 in this conversation — the design history thread stays on one ticket.

## Assistant

You can combine all three — the question is whether the cost is worth it. Here's where the edges are:

**1. Two ways to source the output → which wins?**
If a box has both a literal output value AND a file path configured, the output port has two possible sources. You'd need a precedence rule (literal beats file, or vice versa) and the user has to remember it. The input-port rule today is "wire beats literal" — extending that to outputs is consistent but not obvious at a glance.

**2. Read and write share the same `path`.**
If the box has a `path` configured AND a `write_value` wire AND no output literal, is it reading or writing? You'd pick by presence of `write_value` — wire connected → write mode, no wire → read mode. Workable, but a user who *forgets* to wire the `write_value` ends up with a silent read of the destination file they meant to write to. That's a real footgun.

**3. The output port means different things in each mode.**
- Read mode → emits file contents (possibly large text).
- Write mode → emits `true` / done.
- Literal mode → emits whatever the user typed.

Downstream boxes wired to that port can't statically tell what kind of value is flowing through — same wire, three different semantics. A reader scanning the map sees one node shape but has to read the configuration to know what it does.

**4. Validation surface blows up.**
Three kinds today = three fixed shapes the validator checks. One unified kind = 2³ combinations of (literal? path? write_value?) the validator has to either accept or reject with a clear rule per combo. More edge-case bugs hide there.

**5. Canvas identity disappears.**
Today a green data box looks different from a blue call box at a glance. If one kind can be source-or-sink-or-constant, the badge stops telling the reader what's happening — they have to open the inspector.

---

**A middle ground I'd recommend**: split exactly along the natural seam — *sourcing* vs *sinking*.

- **`data` (source)** — emits one value. The value comes from either an inline literal typed in the inspector OR a file read at `path` (literal or wired). Same output port, same semantics: "here is the value." Choosing literal vs file is a low-stakes mode toggle because both end up as the same kind of thing on the wire.

- **`write` (sink)** — writes an input value to disk at `path`. Emits a done signal. Different action, different look on the canvas.

This collapses your three asks into two kinds while keeping the "I just want to type a value into a box" use case as a first-class feature of the source kind. Reading and emit-literal sit comfortably in one box because they answer the same question. Writing is its own thing because it does something fundamentally different (mutates the filesystem).

Want me to update 229 around that two-kind design (`data` with literal-or-file + `write`)?

read should hide / disable it's input port if a literal value is entered in the inspector.

read should detect the language used by each of it's output wires - remember, each output port can have multiple wires coming out of it. If there's three wires, one that goes to a C box, one that goes the a Lua box, and one that goes to a bash box, then we should output the value as close-to-natively as we can for that language. If there's multiple just do them in a for loop, for each wire. the downstream boxes should treat all read boxes as native.

write should do similar - it should detect which language the input wire is coming from, and use the correct writing procedure defined in the language spec to reduce the friction.

### Tool result

```
1	# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
9	the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
10	`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
11	"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
12	language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
13	table as a data box's value, you either:
14	
15	- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
16	  and lose the Lua syntax niceties; or
17	- Make it a call box that returns the table, paying for a full
18	  spec invocation just to read a constant; or
19	- Hard-code the bytes inline in the box's `data` field, with no
20	  syntactic help from any host language.
21	
22	This is the wrong shape. **A box author writes idiomatic code in
23	the language of their choice.** That's the rule 307 establishes
24	for call boxes: a C-box author writes a plain C function, no
25	SoraMech-specific glue. The data-box equivalent should hold —
26	a Lua-data-box author should write a Lua file that returns
27	whatever they want returned (a table, a string, a number), and
28	SoraMech should know how to lift that into the graph as a JSON
29	wire value without the author writing a single SoraMech-specific
30	character.
31	
32	## Intended behavior
33	
34	Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
35	`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):
36	
37	```c
38	/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
39	 *
40	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle from spec.init.
41	 *   src       — caller-meaningful pointer to the native value.
42	 *               Format is spec-specific (see "Native handle
43	 *               representation" below).
44	 *   src_size  — size hint, or 0 if the spec interprets `src`
45	 *               without a length.
46	 *   out_buf   — destination buffer for JSON text (caller-owned).
47	 *   out_cap   — capacity of out_buf.
48	 *   out_size  — set to the bytes written.
49	 *
50	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. Writes an error message
51	 * via the spec's existing error-reporting plumbing. */
52	int (*native_to_json)(void *ctx,
53	                      const void *src, uint32_t src_size,
54	                      char *out_buf, uint32_t out_cap, uint32_t *out_size);
55	
56	/* Inverse: parse JSON text into a native-language value. The
57	 * native value is stored wherever the spec considers natural
58	 * (Lua: pushed to the worker's lua_State stack; C: written into
59	 * a typed struct supplied by `dst`; Bash: written as a textual
60	 * representation back into dst).
61	 *
62	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle.
63	 *   src       — JSON text.
64	 *   src_size  — JSON text length in bytes.
65	 *   dst       — caller-supplied target; semantics per spec.
66	 *   dst_cap   — capacity, where meaningful.
67	 *   dst_size  — set to the bytes / native units consumed.
68	 *
69	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. */
70	int (*json_to_native)(void *ctx,
71	                      const char *src, uint32_t src_size,
72	                      void *dst, uint32_t dst_cap, uint32_t *dst_size);
73	```
74	
75	These are the same fields 312's same-language fast path
76	reserves; this issue is the one that requires every spec to
77	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
78	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
79	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
80	
81	### The rule, restated
82	
83	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
84	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
85	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
86	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
87	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
88	
89	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
90	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
91	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
92	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
93	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
94	already produced JSON).
95	
96	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
97	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
98	other way around.**
99	
100	## Native handle representation
101	
102	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
103	spec documents what it accepts:
104	
105	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
106	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
107	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
108	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
109	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
110	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
111	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
112	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
113	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
114	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
115	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
116	  JSON shape.)
117	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
118	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
119	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
120	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
121	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
122	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
123	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
124	
125	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
126	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
127	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
128	whole point.
129	
130	## Data box semantics
131	
132	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
133	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
134	
135	```json
136	{
137	  "id": "config",
138	  "kind": "data",
139	  "lang": "lua",
140	  "ref": "config.lua"
141	}
142	```
143	
144	At graph load, the runner:
145	
146	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
147	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
148	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
149	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
150	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
151	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
152	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
153	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
154	   value to JSON text.
155	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
156	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
157	   from a language-native file.
158	
159	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
160	inline-data path applies unchanged.
161	
162	## Why every spec must implement these
163	
164	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
165	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
166	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
167	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
168	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
169	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
170	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
171	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
172	both so any language can play any role.
173	
174	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
175	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
176	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
177	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
178	loud rather than producing wrong output.
179	
180	## Suggested implementation steps
181	
182	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
183	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
184	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
185	   reserved.)
186	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
187	   spec:
188	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
189	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
190	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
191	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
192	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
193	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
194	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
195	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
196	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
197	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
198	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
199	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
200	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

Issue 229 is updated. Key points captured:

**Two kinds**: `read` + `write`. No unified `data` source.

**`read`**:
- Default: `path` input → reads file, emits contents.
- Inline literal value in inspector → emits that, hides the `path` input port on canvas.
- Per-downstream-wire native conversion: for each outgoing wire, look up the target box's language and use that spec's `json_to_native` to lift the value to native form. A Lua consumer gets a Lua table; a C consumer gets a C struct; a Bash consumer gets text.

**`write`**:
- Inputs: `path` + `value`. Output: `true` boolean done signal (optional, discarded when unwired).
- Per-upstream-wire native conversion: ask the source language's spec to canonicalize via `native_to_json`, write JSON bytes atomically.
- Color: orange cheddar (`#d4762a`).
